#132. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#130. Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Nobody will be celebrating the fact that prices in the UK are rising at the fastest pace for a year.

Add to that an expectation of higher household energy bills and sharpening price increases and things look grim for everyone.

But there are some signs of hope that may take the edge off the rising cost of living. Here's how.

That headline is not missing a decimal point. The latest prediction for the increase in domestic energy prices in the new year is, indeed, 26%.

Millions of homes in England, Scotland and Wales are on tariffs affected by regulator Ofgem's price cap, which is set every three months. The next change for January will be officially announced next Wednesday, 25 November.

Energy consultancy Cornwall Insight, which has a good track record with its predictions, suggests that a household using a typical amount of gas and electricity will pay Â£2,166 a year - that's an increase of Â£443 from current levels.

Not all energy use for the year is between January and March, but a big chunk is owing to the cold, dark days and nights.

How much you pay depends on your use, with heavy users of gas likely to be the hardest hit.

The government has promised a Â£100 payment for people living in homes in council tax bands A to D in England to help cover winter energy costs. Other nations are planning similar support.

The latest inflation data shows that government assistance can have an impact on the general rate of inflation, external, as recorded by the Office for National Statistics (ONS).

Domestic energy prices rose by nearly 4% in October, but this was offset in inflationary terms by a cut in VAT on electricity bills.

While the rate of inflation in general was 3.6% in the year to October, food prices only went up by 0.9%.

It is partly the result of intense competition between the supermarkets. Like many retailers, they are facing higher costs but are not passing that onto their customers in full.

There may be some scars from the recent cost-of-living spike here. Shoppers suffered a period during the big rise in inflation in 2022-23 when food prices rocketed.

Those higher prices are embedded. While food prices overall aren't coming down now, they aren't rising anywhere near as fast as back then.

The detail here is very interesting. Take chocolate: world cocoa prices are down since 2024, and now chocolate prices are falling - down 2.9%, according to the latest data.

But the ONS has also pointed to cat food as one, albeit small, driver of the faster rate of rising prices.

Should high energy and fuel costs - which are driving the rising rate of inflation - persist, there are questions over whether the cost of producing and transporting food also goes up, and the chances of that higher cost being passed on to consumers are greater.

Economists have been closely studying whether there are any signs of those so-called second-round effects - where an initial price shock spreads to wages, services and broader consumer prices. So far, there are relatively few.

If prices rise then it takes a bigger chunk of people's income and so has a deeper impact on those whose finances are already stretched, so the latest data on food prices is good news for many people.

Typical energy bill forecast to go up by Â£443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

One set of economists who will consider whether there is evidence of second-round effects are the nine people who set interest rates, which heavily influences the cost of borrowing money - such as mortgages - as well as the returns people get on savings.

The Bank of England's Monetary Policy Committee meets again in December and, after that, in February.

Having just raised interest rates - for the first time in more than three years - to 4%, there's a widespread expectation among analysts that another hike is to come.

To be clear, we don't know if and when that will happen for sure.

The case for an increase would be if prices continue to accelerate. The Bank will want to slow that down.

The case against would be price rises remaining relatively confined to energy-related costs. Any easing of Middle East tensions could limit price rises without Bank of England intervention.

As far as wages are concerned, the latest official data suggests that rising prices are largely covered by rising pay.

That is an average - so is not true for everyone - but how long it remains the case may be key to how deep upcoming price rises cut into our personal finances.

How is the rising cost of living affecting your quality of life?

#128. Palo Alto inks $4.5 billion deal for AI startup Applied Compute, shares pop 7% on earnings

Palo Alto Networks shares jumped more than 7% after the cybersecurity firm beat Wall Street's fiscal first-quarter estimates and inked a deal for artificial intelligence startup Applied Compute.

Here's how the company did versus LSEG estimates:

Revenue jumped 39% from $2.47 billion in the year-ago quarter.

Fears of AI-powered cyberattacks, heightened by incidents like the Hugging Face breach, are driving demand for cybersecurity vendors like Palo Alto Networks. Amid this backdrop, the company's shares have jumped 85% this year.

"As AI reshapes the threat landscape, we must continually evolve to protect our customers and enable them to adopt AI securely," CEO Nikesh Arora said in a release.

Palo Alto said annual recurring revenue for its next-generation security business jumped 66% from a year ago to $9.73 billion. Arora said the NGS ARR was one of the highest beats in the company's history.

The cybersecurity company bumped up guidance for fiscal year 2027 as businesses spend more on security in the age of AI.

Palo Alto expects adjusted earnings per share in the range of $4.26 to $4.30 and revenue in the range of $14.52 billion to $14.57 billion. The forecasts topped the earnings of $4.18 per share and $14.16 billion in revenue expected by analysts.

Net income totaled $482 million, or 57 cents per share, up from $334 million, or 47 cents per share, a year ago.

Palo Alto Networks has been steadily acquiring companies to capitalize on demand for cyber tools in the age of AI.

Arora said the company's $4.5 billion all-stock deal for Applied Compute will help businesses secure open-weight models as adoption grows. The startup helps businesses run and customize their own AI models and marks a major investment for Palo Alto beyond cybersecurity.

"If it works, I think this will dwarf the ability that we have in cybersecurity," Arora said on an earnings call. "This could be bigger than what we do."

Outages from Microsoft, banks and air traffic control have heightened concerns around cyberattacks over the last week. U.S. officials have so far ruled out any malicious activity, but experts say the rapid acceleration in patching is straining dated systems.

As businesses discover more vulnerabilities, Arora said the company expects tens of thousands of virtual patches by the end of the fiscal year. "Whatever they do today is going to take three to six months, maybe nine months, depending on how complex and how tired their environment is," Arora said.

#127. Typical energy bill forecast to go up by £443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

Gas and electricity bills for a typical household are forecast to jump by Â£443 a year from January as the inflation rate rose to its highest level in a year.

A typical annual bill is predicted to rise by 26% in the new year, according to consultancy Cornwall Insight, ahead of the official announcement next week.

Some households will get a Â£100 payment to help with the rising cost of living, a policy which was announced in the Budget.

Prices rose 3.6% in the year to October, up from 3.5% in September mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices, official figures show.

Inflation has been driven higher in recent months as the US-Israel war with Iran has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

In response to prices surging, the Bank of England raised interest rates for the first time in three years earlier this month, in a bid to slow inflation to its target of 2%.

Interest rates increased from 3.75% to 4%, and the Bank signalled a further rise could be likely unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved.

Higher interest rates lift the cost of borrowing for some mortgages, loans and credit cards, but can also lead to a better return on savings.

Emma Reynolds, chief secretary to the Treasury, said the government was providing help for households with energy costs.

VAT has been cut on electricity bills to zero until next July and the government will also hand Â£100 to 20 million homes in January.

But with annual household energy bills forecast to reach Â£2,166, Cornwall Insight's retail markets principal, Craig Lowrey, said that "ultimately this support can only chip away at the edges of a bill that continues to be pushed up by events in the Middle East".

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh, said: "Affordability is my number one priority, and I will continue driving towards cheap, clean power."

Sophie, who manages a cafe in Brighton, told the BBC she watches her smart meter at home "like a hawk".

"I'm very conscious of the cost of energy, and it is having an impact on how we function in the home as a family," she said.

"I try to cook using the oven a lot less than I used to. We haven't got the heating on yet in our household. We're just like 'wear more layers'. We've got electric blankets on the sofa."

Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Shadow chancellor, Andrew Griffith, said four successive rises in the inflation rate was down to the UK's "soaring energy costs" and Labour's decision to "hike taxes and employment costs".

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the ONS, said fuel prices were "once again a major contributor" to the increase in the inflation rate in October.

Rising energy prices can lead to more expensive food and staples for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs.

Earlier this month, the Bank of England said there had been "limited evidence" of this, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower profit margins.

But Fitzner said on Wednesday: "Annual costs of both raw materials and goods leaving factories continue to climb, driven by higher crude oil and petrol prices, respectively."

Petrol prices rose by 5.2p per litre between September and October, the ONS said, with the average price last month hitting 174.7p per litre - the highest since August 2022.

Diesel prices, meanwhile, reached the highest price since records began 30 years ago, averaging 199.5p per litre in October.

But the ONS said price inflation in restaurants and cafes had slowed last month, and that prices had fallen on average for hotels.

And food price inflation rose by 0.9%, the slowest pace in five years.

Core inflation, which strips out energy and food costs, and services inflation were little changed on last month. The figure is closely watched by the Bank of England when setting interest rates.

It could mean the Bank opts to hold off on a further rate rise in December, though policymakers believe inflation could peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year and not return to 2% until 2028.

In an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil prices higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it has suggested.

A spokesperson for the Bank said it decided to raise rates two weeks ago to "limit how much higher energy costs affect other prices, and will continue to set interest rates to make sure inflation returns to the 2% target and stays there".

Additional reporting by Raphael Sheridan, economics producer

#125. Hims & Hers launches new peptide treatments — while admitting there's more to learn about efficacy

Hims & Hers Health on Wednesday said it will offer five formulations of peptides and longevity treatments compounded in the U.S. as part of the initial rollout of its Peptides+ platform, all of which are not approved by regulators.

Even as it announced the new products, Hims & Hers acknowledged it has more to learn about how best to use the peptides and how effective they are — underscoring the uncertainty surrounding the growing space.

The new offerings come after the federal government eased restrictions on compounding certain unapproved peptides, which are short chains of amino ​acids. Those products have exploded in popularity among fitness and longevity enthusiasts who attest that they help with muscle growth, rapid injury recovery and anti-aging, even though most lack evidence from robust human trials.

Hims in late October launched an early access list for the upcoming peptide offerings, and more than 10,000 people signed up within 15 days, a company spokesperson said in a statement.

None of the company's specific compounded products are approved by the Food and Drug Administration. But Hims said they are authorized by law and formulated by pharmacies "that adhere to rigorous quality standards and are licensed and inspected by state boards of pharmacy."

The products include compounded formulations of sermorelin, a peptide that stimulates growth-hormone release and is marketed for recovery, body composition and anti-aging; glutathione, a peptide promoted ‌for anti-aging and athletic recovery; and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide, or NAD+, a coenzyme that plays a role in cellular metabolism.

The company will also offer compounded versions of vitamin B-12, a vitamin needed to make red blood cells, build DNA, and maintain healthy nerve function, and L-carnitine, an amino acid derivative that helps the body turn fat into energy. The FDA has approved certain prescription forms of both peptides for established medical uses, primarily for diagnosed deficiencies in those compounds.

Hims said it plans to add more peptides and therapies made in the U.S. over time. That includes BPC-157, an unapproved peptide derived from a protein found in human gastric juice that is marketed for healing processes. The company also aims to introduce another unapproved peptide called TB-500, which is derived from a naturally occurring protein in the body and is marketed for broader, systemic soft-tissue recovery and flexibility. Most of the research on both has been conducted in animals, not humans.

To receive the products, members on the Hims platform will start with an assessment of health history and goals with a licensed provider trained in peptide therapies, who can tailor a personalized plan to their needs, if appropriate.

The company said it intends to use its communication platform to check in with patients and ensure they are seeing desired results.

"If they're not, we need to do something about it," Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, Hims chief medical officer, told CNBC. "We need to, you know, adjust the dose when that's appropriate, or in some cases consider other compounds that's maybe a better fit. And of course, there will also be cases when we need to refer people to other care options."

However, Vinjamoori said there's still uncertainty over how to best use the peptides.

"We don't have all of this data that we're used to having with compounds like this," he said. "We have the opportunity to get it right, and I think we see that as our responsibility with a company of our size, our skill, and resources to really lead."

Vinjamoori added that people who have serious illnesses, such as cardiovascular disease, liver disease and kidney disease, will not be candidates for the compounds for now.

Meanwhile, Andrew Huberman, a well known neuroscientist, podcaster and Stanford University School of Medicine professor who recently joined Hims' board of directors, in a statement called compounds "real medical treatments, not just a wellness trend." He said Hims provides safe access through "trusted sourcing and physician oversight."

But health experts raised concerns about the broader mainstream marketing of peptides in the U.S. and accessing them on telehealth platforms.

"With peptides, I think you have to view them the same way you would view any drug that hasn't been rigorously tested," said Joshua Kritzer, a chemistry professor at Tufts University. "Without actual testing, all you have is anecdotal information, and that's just not enough information to put a substance in your body."

Kritzer said the lack of large clinical trials means researchers can't rule out potentially serious, rare complications from peptides — the kinds of risks that have led regulators to reject or pull otherwise effective drugs from the market after they underwent comprehensive testing in humans.

Without that testing, it's also unclear how telehealth firms, physicians and pharmacies can make decisions about what doses patients should take, said Paul Knoepfler, a cell biology and human anatomy professor at UC Davis Health. While some people rely on anecdotal data from real peptide users, "the dosing for these products really has not been established," he added.

Knoepfler said one risk is that injecting too much of a synthetic peptide into patients could trigger "weird immune responses that are really bad for one's health" because the body might recognize it as foreign.

"With the peptides, it's very loosey-goosey," Knoepfler told CNBC. "How does one even know what would be the effective amount for whatever you're hoping it will do, and what amount would have more risks?"

But he said the more contact patients can have with physicians around taking a compounded peptide, the better. Providers, including those tied to telehealth firms, should make sure patients are well-informed about the possible risks and unknowns of a given product and consider their preexisting conditions, cancer history and other medications, Knoepfler said.

That appears to be consistent with the approach Hims & Hers is taking. The company says patients who use the peptide and longevity products can also use its Care+ offering, which pairs treatment with ongoing support. That includes twice-a-year lab testing to track their progress and the ability to message providers trained in peptide therapy around the clock with any questions.

Still, Kritzer argued that having a doctor who only knows a patient from online, essentially mail-order platforms or websites "is not enough supervision to put you on a prescription medication," especially an unapproved one.

Hims' ability to legally sell some of these peptides stems from a major shift in federal oversight by the Health and Human Services Department. It issued a policy in October that says the Food and Drug Administration will not take enforcement action against licensed pharmacies, federal facilities or physicians that compound and dispense six unapproved peptides while formal rules are reviewed.

In practice, that means these pharmacies and other firms can now offer them, even though the peptides aren't yet on the FDA's official list of ingredients compounding pharmacies are allowed to make. It does not translate to formal approval of the specific peptides.

The policy followed a 2023 decision by the Biden administration where the FDA restricted compounding pharmacies from producing popular peptide formulations due to safety, impurity and immune-reaction concerns.

HHS Secretary ​Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has supported broader access to peptides, calling himself a "big fan" of the treatments and arguing that restrictions were driving Americans to potentially unsafe products from overseas. Kennedy has previously described using BPC-157 to treat a muscle injury.

The announcement also comes after Hims in February 2025 acquired a California-based peptide manufacturing facility equipped with advanced peptide synthesizers.

The evidence for peptide efficacy varies by compound.

A 2026 systematic review screening 113 studies, including both small trials on humans along with rodent research, found zero eligible clinical outcome trials proving that administering IV or intramuscular NAD+ improves anti-aging, energy, or wellness endpoints in humans.

There is some evidence for specific uses of certain peptides, such as potential weight loss. A review of 37 L-carnitine trials found participants lost about 2.7 pounds on average, though that analysis does not demonstrate that a compounded injection works the same way.

Meanwhile, vitamin B-12 injections are most commonly used to help people with a diagnosed deficiency rather than preventative care. U.S. survey data shows only 3.6% of adults are B-12 deficient.

Glutathione's evidence is thin, too. IV injections of it are widely marketed for skin whitening, yet not FDA-approved for this purpose.

A small study published in the Journal of Pakistan Association of Dermatologists evaluating IV glutathione showed that the treatment is "not very effective" for skin-lightening, and that it loses efficacy with time.

The FDA also said in August that it received reports of at least 30 patients who experienced adverse events after they received compounded IV glutathione alone or in combination with other ingredients from different compounding pharmacies.

All of the glutathione tied to the reports were from the same wholesale distributor and was labeled as dietary supplement grade, which is not an appropriate ingredient to make injectable drugs. Two of the Texas pharmacies conducted recalls related to elevated endotoxin levels in their glutathione injectable drugs.

These studies and reports don't speak to how Hims' exact combination will perform, but they do highlight the current limits on data studying the treatments and the questions surrounding them.

#124. Why has Waymo pulled all its driverless cars from US freeways?

Waymo has temporarily suspended driverless rides on US freeways after California banned the service over safety concerns.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles said a series of traffic incidents, including robotaxis entering active construction zones, posed an imminent risk to public safety. Waymo has now restricted freeway operations in all other US markets while it reviews the performance of its vehicles.

Waymo currently operates in 15 US cities, though only offered driverless freeway rides in a handful of cities, including San Francisco and Los Angeles.

#122. I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

SHENZHEN, China — It took a trip to China for me, an American, to see U.S. President Donald Trump in person for the first time.

Trump emerged from a group of black-clad photographers and other personnel surrounding him at a welcome banquet in Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting held Nov. 17 to 19.

Seated just a few spots to his left was Russian President Vladimir Putin who picked up a glass of red wine to join a toast led by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

"At tomorrow's Leaders' Meeting, we will discuss measures to expand mutual opening up, explore pathways to innovation-driven growth, and identify opportunities to expand practical cooperation across various fields, so as to translate more initiatives into tangible results," Xi said in Mandarin, according to a CNBC translation.

Xi was speaking broadly, but Thursday's agenda includes a bilateral meeting with Trump, and their highly anticipated trilateral meeting with Putin.

But looking at the mood in the room, it seemed that their trilateral conversation had already started Wednesday night at their long banquet table. Much of what happens at APEC, including bilateral meetings and other exchanges between leaders, takes place away from the media.

But what struck me was how little sign there was of the tension surrounding Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang resignation, a controversy that has gripped markets for the past 10 days.

Ahead of Trump's entrance into the banquet hall, Chinese Commerce Minister Wang Wentao shared a friendly moment with U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and other members of the American delegation.

Last week, the U.S. officials said that Trump did not plan to raise allegations of Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy during his meeting with Xi.

It was a striking contrast to just two months ago, when I saw photographs of Huang and Tesla CEO Elon Musk sharing a table with Trump and Xi in Washington, D.C.

And when Trump visited Beijing in May, Huang took a few minutes to speak with reporters, including me, inside the Great Hall of the People. Musk was there too, accompanied by one of his sons.

This time, neither were visible in a room that was closely watched by the world. Musk was expected to join Trump's delegation in Shenzhen, but I did not see him Wednesday evening.

Their absence from the government-heavy banquet was striking. The world's most powerful leaders may be grappling with the implications of artificial intelligence, but they certainly didn't need the industry's biggest names at the table to do so.

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

Over the months I've spent following APEC's "China Year," one thing has become clear: The forum has given Beijing an opportunity to host the U.S., Japan and other member economies at a time when unresolved tensions might otherwise have made such gatherings difficult.

But even in a year defined by talks of U.S.-China "strategic stability," the APEC events have exposed the rifts and the shifting dynamics in global power. Those dynamics were on display for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

Several hundred attendees, from Xiaomi CEO Lei Jun to U.S. Ambassador to China David Perdue, arrived early for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

After waiting in silence for at least 10 minutes, the audience welcomed Xi in a distinctly modern way: by raising their smartphones to capture the moment. The applause was rather muted.

It was much the same at the more government-heavy welcome banquet that evening. So many attendees rushed to take their own photographs of Xi and other leaders entering the room that reporters' views were largely blocked.

Following Xi's morning speech, there was a lengthy intermission before Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim took the stage.

"The entire multilateral framework needs to change," he said, praising China on its "rare" recognition of smaller countries.

During the tea break that followed, attendees quickly resumed mingling. Some even brought coffee and snacks back into the room as Putin prepared to speak.

They listened as the Russian president talked about his country's economic resilience and claimed Europe was losing ground. As attendees headed to lunch, one thing stood out: Neither Trump nor any other G7 leader had taken the stage at the opening event. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney spoke later in the afternoon.

The most visible American presence during the morning session came from business leaders appearing in a promotional video ahead of Xi's speech.

And the absence of an American voice was perhaps even more noticeable at Wednesday evening's banquet.

Trump did not follow Xi with a toast of his own. This was the same famously outspoken president, who in a winding speech in Davos, had declared that the U.S. must get Greenland.

Yet on Wednesday night in China, Trump wasn't the one holding the microphone.

#120. Europe plans to catch the next computer chip wave

Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data

"Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough," says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.

If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.

The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.

They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).

Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.

Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.

"With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago."

American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.

Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent "a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips".

"Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house'," says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.

"We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing."

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently.

"[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer," says van Mol.

His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.

It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.

"That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.

"In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it."

Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment

Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.

The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.

Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.

"The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip," says van Mol.

"You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology."

ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.

"Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing," says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.

Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a "launchpad" for European tech

ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.

The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.

In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as "a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy".

"By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction," he said.

Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.

In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called "the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.

The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.

It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.

Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.

If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.

"Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible", Teer says.

"To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key."

Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.

It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.

"What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product," Ton van Mol says.

Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.

It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.

Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.

For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.

"The demand is there", says van Mol, "and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom."

#119. Gilts v kilts: What is happening with Scottish government bonds?

The Scottish government is facing questions about its plan to issue its first ever bonds.

The move would allow ministers to borrow money from investors who in return receive regular interest payments.

The aim is to raise about Â£1.5bn by the end of the parliamentary term, with the funds to be used on long-term projects such as building hospitals or maintaining roads.

Named "kilts", a nod to UK government "gilts", the scheme has provoked much debate.

This week, MSPs on Holyrood's finance committee heard about the potential risks and rewards.

A bond is a financial tool used by a government to borrow money. They are sold with a promise for the buyer, or investor, to be paid back at the end of an agreed term.

The government will also make regular payments to the investor.

In the UK, a government bond is called a gilt. They are typically bought by financial institutions such as pension funds, insurance companies and commercial banks.

As a play on this term, the Scottish bonds have been nicknamed "kilts".

The Scottish government has said it expects the bonds to be issued within this financial year, subject to market conditions.

Former First Minister Humza Yousaf commissioned initial work in 2023 after advisers in the Scottish government's Investor Panel recommended making bonds available to the market as a means of raising Scotland's profile and attracting investment.

In November last year, the government announced that it had received an identical credit rating to the UK's score.

This was based on "prudent fiscal management" and the country's economic stability - though ministers were warned this could change if Scotland moved towards independence.

With market volatility due to conflicts in Ukraine and the Middle East, some have questioned whether now is a good time to launch bonds.

But at Holyrood, David McMillan, professor in finance and accounting at the University of Stirling, told MSPs: "You can't wait for a perfect time because there's going to be no perfect time."

The government wants to raise Â£1.5bn through bonds by 2031, although that would only be a fraction of its near Â£70bn budget.

About four-fifths of the Scottish government's budget is used on resource spending, which goes towards day-to-day costs such as funding the NHS, benefits payments and paying salaries.

A smaller portion is made up of capital spending, typically used on long-term physical assets such as buildings and bridges, or maintaining roads or paths.

The Scottish government is unable to borrow for resource spending other than to correct forecasting errors.

However, for capital spending, it can borrow as much as Â£450m a year at 2023-24 levels up to a total cap of Â£3bn - to be uprated with inflation.

Adjusted for inflation, the government could borrow about Â£491m in the current financial year, with a total limit of about Â£3.3bn.

Economists have noted that Scottish bonds would be a more costly way of borrowing than existing routes provided by the UK government through the National Loans Fund.

It has been estimated, external that interest rates on "kilts" would be 0.3 percentage points above gilts, plus an additional Â£2m per bond in administrative costs.

The Scottish government has not released costings, citing market sensitivities, but has insisted, external issuing bonds would represent value for money relative to the costs of borrowing through the UK scheme.

Ministers argue that issuing bonds would be a good opportunity to market the country to investors, and would be helpful if Scotland became independent.

Dr Ben Cormier, a senior lecturer at the University of Strathclyde's department of government and public policy, told Holyrood's finance committee that while the Scottish bonds could cost marginally more than the UK scheme, they would "bring flexibility to public finances and debt management".

McMillan agreed with the plans "in principle", but warned they would lose "credibility" with investors if limited to five years.

Cormier warned that independence was the one thing "that could materially change the nature of risk associated with Scottish bonds".

Conservative MSP Liam Kerr said bonds could be more expensive than borrowing through the National Loans Fund and voiced concerns about a lack of transparency in the programme.

McMillan agreed there should be "full information" available, saying: "You've got to have a clear, full and frank conversation with the public."

#117. Elon Musk says record $446m spending on US midterms was 'pretty foolish'

Elon Musk said on Wednesday that he felt "pretty foolish" about the record sum he spent on the US midterm elections, following sweeping Republican defeats.

The Tesla and SpaceX chief executive was the election's top individual donor, giving $446m (Â£339m) to support President Donald Trump's party, particularly in Texas where many of Musk's firms are based.

Musk, who had backed Trump's second White House bid, also said he was "not Maga" - the Make America Great Again movement associated with the president.

Democrats won control of the both chambers in the US Congress, flipping both swing seats and making historic gains in places that have for decades been controlled by Republicans.

The election was seen as a bruising defeat for Trump, who has faced pressure over the impact his policies have had on the cost of living in the US.

Musk was speaking virtually, external at the New York Times DealBook Summit, an annual conference hosted in the city attended by major business and political leaders.

"I'm not Maga," Musk said, referring to the right-wing political movement.

He added: "I'm not sure what Maga means... if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don't have that."

This marks a major shift in tone by Musk, who has previously been seen wearing a Maga hat.

"I'm not just Maga, I'm dark Maga," he joked at a Trump rally in 2024.

Musk has worn a Maga hat in public on several occasions

For several months at the start of Trump's second term in the White House, Musk served as an unpaid "special government employee" and Senior Advisor to the President, leading the Department of Government Efficiency (Doge) to cut government spending.

In October 2026, the Pentagon named Musk as a co-lead of a future of warfare study called Project Meridian, while he continued to travel with and advise Trump.

Musk went on to say he did not see much for the money he had spent on the Republicans' campaign, though he added that it was likely that candidates would have fared worse without the spending.

The large number of Republican losses could have implications for Musk and his businesses.

Democrats coming into power in both the Senate and House of Representatives have pledged greater scrutiny of tech companies, including over the building of controversial data centres.

In Georgia, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the newly elected Democrat governor promised to limit the rollout of the massive buildings, as well as the tax breaks tech companies often get to build them.

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested that SpaceX, which has gained an estimated $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was re-elected, should be investigated.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

One of the most striking midterms wins went to Democrat James Talarico, who beat Republican Ken Paxton for a US Senate seat in Texas.

According to BBC analysis, Musk had spent at least $45m in support of Paxton.

Texas is where key operations for Musk's car company Tesla and rocket and technology firm SpaceX are based.

Musk said on Wednesday that he believes Texas' new governor, Democrat Gina Hinojosa, is "going to be quite moderate".

Hinojosa defeated Republican Governor Greg Abbott, ending more than three decades of the party's control of the state.

The Tesla boss added : "I don't think she's a far-left governor. And based on everything I've heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas with Gina as governor."

#115. Ford starts U.S. battery cell production using Chinese tech amid geopolitical tensions

MARSHALL, Mich. — Ford Motor has started producing battery cells in the U.S. for its next generation of electric vehicles using technologies licensed by Chinese battery giant CATL.

The automaker said Wednesday that cell production at a $3 billion plant in Marshall, Michigan — roughly 100 miles west of Detroit — started last month and the cells are now shipping out. They'll be used in prototypes for its Fathom small electric pickup truck, which CEO Jim Farley has touted as rivaling Tesla and Chinese competitors.

The production marks a milestone for the Detroit automaker and is the next step toward using lower-cost lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, battery cells in U.S. vehicles. The technology was created in the U.S. but commercialized largely in China.

The automaker's decision to make the batteries at the roughly 2 million-square-foot facility has been criticized by some lawmakers and U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over Ford's ties to CATL.

Alan Clarke, Ford's vice president of advanced development projects, said licensing the technology from CATL helped the automaker bring the low-cost battery chemistry to the U.S. as quickly as possible.

"If we want to get to being able to produce affordable EVs in the United States, we need to also do it the fastest way possible," Clarke said Tuesday during a tour of the facility. "CATL being the biggest in the world means that they're the best supplier to get us that technology and help us learn ourselves how to produce LFP cells."

The U.S. government has increased scrutiny of Chinese ties with U.S. companies even as President Donald Trump is meeting this week with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

In September, Duffy sent a letter to Farley expressing "profound concern" about Ford's ties to Chinese companies that it believes could be detrimental to the Detroit carmaker and the U.S. automotive industry. The White House a day later followed up by commending the automaker for its domestic jobs and manufacturing.

Ford — the largest producer of U.S. vehicles — has routinely touted its American roots, jobs and manufacturing, especially as the Biden and Trump administrations have pushed to onshore and grow U.S. production.

Ford's battery plant in Marshall is expected to be capable of producing 40 battery cells a minute, equating to 20 gigawatt-hours annually for EVs and the company's energy storage business. That's down from initial plans of up to 40 gigawatt-hours annually amid an EV pullback by the automaker and broader industry.

Ford has said the production pace will help reduce battery costs and that the facility has room for additional capacity if needed.

The facility is highly automated, but Ford said it's still in the early stages of certifying its processes, as it plans to ramp up cell production next year before the expected launch of the Fathom pickup during the fourth quarter of 2027.

#113. The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

Brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei are chief executive and president of Anthropic

In the space of just five years, Anthropic has gone from unknown start-up to tech giant, worth more than a trillion dollars.

The company behind the AI platform Claude was formed by seven people who left rival OpenAI in 2021 and, in a matter of hours, will become multi-billionaires - on paper at least.

Anthropic is floating the business on the US stock exchange and will sell its shares at $83 each, it said on Wednesday.

At that price, the founders' shareholdings will be worth at least $19.3bn (Â£14.7bn) each.

So, who are these people, all in their thirties and forties, who are about to become incredibly wealthy?

In 2020, Dario Amodei was working for OpenAI, the firm that brought generative artificial intelligence to a mainstream audience, when it launched Chat-GPT.

He decided he wanted to do things his own way, and left, taking his sister Daniela, and five colleagues with him.

Amodei grew up in San Francisco, impressing school friends not just with his academic prowess but also his speed on the running track, and his preoccupation with philosophical and technological questions including the potential of machines to gain consciousness and human longevity.

Initially he studied physics at Princeton and Stanford. But following his father's death from hepatitis C, he switched to biology and biomedical sciences.

Amodei did stints at the Chinese tech giant Baidu and Google before joining OpenAI.

But he began to worry that some people within the company were putting profit before safety, prompting the decision to jump ship.

Now 43, he is Anthropic's chief executive, and the person who, in his sister's words, makes the company "ambitious".

Dario and Daniela Amodei, were close growing up and later shared a flat together in San Francisco with other friends. She followed him into OpenAI and joined him when he left to found Anthropic.

She and her brother have always genuinely liked each other, she says, but the only thing she ever did first, was when he followed her in becoming vegetarian.

Amodei studied English Literature at the University of California Santa Cruz, going on to work for Democrat representative Matt Cartwright.

She has also worked at financial services platform Stripe, where she was head of risk operations.

The 39-year-old is president of Anthropic, manages the executive team, and leads the firm's research, engineering, product, governance and commercial work.

Tom Brown, now 39, followed the Amodei siblings when they left to found their new venture.

He led GPT-3 engineering at OpenAI, and prior to that was at Google on its DeepMind project.

Brown established his entrepreneurial credentials co-founding an app called "Grouper" that helps set up drinks between groups of friends.

He has supported US President Donald Trump's stance on datacentres, arguing that they are "an enormous source of prosperity".

Now as chief compute officer at Anthropic he is responsible for how the company secures and uses its computing resources.

Jack Clark, is another arts graduate among the original start-up's founding members and the only one who is not from North America.

Originally from Brighton in the UK, he studied English Literature and Creative Writing at the University of East Anglia and became a technology journalist, writing for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

He was caught up in the excitement of artificial intelligence and joined OpenAI, where he worked initially in communications, later becoming its policy director.

Now, 37, Clark is head of public benefit at Anthropic. He also leads the Anthropic Institute, an AI think tank.

Jared Kaplan, 42, is chief science officer at Anthropic, and heads its technical research. He is also an associate professor at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore.

He holds a PhD in physics from Harvard University.

His work on scaling laws - which describe mathematical patterns that show that bigger AI models trained on more data tend to be more consistent - has helped provide AI firms with a framework for predicting AI behaviour, according to Anthropic.

Chris Olah is described as "interpretability research lead" primarily focused on how AI models work with each other.

But according to the New York Times, Olah has spent much of the last year convening meetings with faith leaders from across the religious spectrum.

Described by the newspaper as "boyish" but with "the zeal of an evangelist" the 34-year-old is reportedly exploring the relationship between morality and AI.

He previously led interoperability research at OpenAI, and prior to that was a research scientist at Google Brain.

The Canadian left the University of Toronto early, but later secured a Thiel Fellowship of $100,000, which is a scholarship to help gifted people move into research without a degree.

Sam McCandlish, 36, takes less of a public-facing role than the other founders, but like them will own around 1.6% of the company after its shares go on sale. As a result he too will see his stake crystallise into a hugely valuable asset.

McCandlish is Anthropic's chief architect, and helps shape and implement the company's policy designed to mitigate the risks of AI. Prior to this he worked on safety at OpenAI.

He has a physics PhD from Stanford, and maths and physics degree from Brandeis University outside Boston.

#112. Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

It was during November 2020 that somewhere in San Francisco, a woman called Daniela Amodei phoned a friend, asking for advice.

Daniela oversaw safety and policy at OpenAI, one of the most talked-about AI companies in the world.

But Daniela and her brother Dario - who worked there too - had misgivings over safety levels at OpenAI and thought it could be time to walk away.

So she did what many do when we can't make a decision about something, she phoned a friend. The friend's reply was direct and to the point: "You don't need to be on the phone with me. You already know the answer."

The Amodei siblings and a handful of other colleagues quit. Together, they set up a rival â Anthropic.

Six years on and Anthropic, the maker of leading AI model Claude, has predicted revenues of $52bn in 2026 and $135bn in 2027, according to analyst estimates.

It is about to sell shares to the public for the first time in what's known as an Initial Public Offering or IPO, aiming for a valuation of an estimated $1.4tn (Â£1tn), which would propel it straight into the top 10 of the most valuable US companies.

The sale comes at a crucial time when many are wondering whether, amid increasing concern about the potential of AI, the industry's bubble is showing signs of bursting.

Where once investors were "all in" on a technological revolution to rival the railroads, the car, electricity and the internet, some are now hedging their bets, amid arguments that AI prices will be driven down as the technology becomes more common and concerns that a hefty wedge of the AI investment is tech giants investing in each other.

Shares in companies that supply the raw computing power behind AI, like Coreweave and Nebius, have seen their value cut in half while even the mighty Nvidia has lost its crown as the world's most valuable company with its shares down 30% - a staggering $2tn fall in value.

So could the Anthropic IPO reinflate the bubble or will it be remembered as the last gasp of a boom that is heading for a bust?

At an event last week, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told JPMorgan Chase's CEO Jamie Dimon that his guiding principle was to "never let your company be worth a single dollar more than it's worth".

The company is selling roughly $65bn worth of shares to the general public and a further $26bn on to other big tech companies. It is comfortably the single biggest money raising event in US corporate history.

It's an old adage that during a gold rush you should buy companies that sell the picks and shovels as opposed to those staking everything on striking gold. In AI's case that's the chip makers, the data centre builders, the energy providers. But Anthropic is arguably the gold itself and this IPO will see what value people put on it, according to Fiona Cincotta.

"I think the reason that this IPO is so important is basically it's the first ability for the public investors to access a pure play AI trade," she says, adding that previous offerings have been about "other parts of the ecosystem, which are, the semiconductors, the chips, the, data centres. But this is the first time that we were actually able to access the AI provider."

Daniela Amodei helped set up a rival company to OpenAI

What a company is worth depends almost entirely on predicted future earnings.

It's that calculation that many think presents the sharpest needle to the AI bubble.

Will current and future users of AI be prepared to pay enough to justify the trillions being spent on the chips, data centres, energy and employees, some of whom can command annual salaries of over $100m?

"AI is certainly in a financial bubble. While LLMs [large language models] offer genuine utility and are undoubtedly here to stay, the economics don't add up," said Gary Marcus, Professor Emeritus at New York University and author of Taming Silicon Valley, speaking earlier this year.

"[The] more companies build AI models, the [more the] technology becomes commoditised, eliminating meaningful competitive advantages and driving down prices."

So far, the growth rates in Anthropic revenue are extraordinary.

But these explosive growth rates hide a potential problem. Around a quarter of that revenue came from just a handful of companies, among them Google and Amazon.

It's also concentrated in the US which accounts for two-thirds of its revenue.

There is also the staggering network of circular financing found in the AI world. For example, Anthropic has agreements to buy or lease hundreds of billions of dollars worth of chips and computing power from a handful of companies including Broadcom, Google, Amazon. Many of those firms are also investors in Anthropic and major customers of its products.

It's not just some Wall Street investors questioning whether the AI boom is all it's cracked up to be. Out on Main Street, ordinary Americans have concerns too.

With the US driving so much of global AI growth, a growing public backlash against a technology that threatens to replace millions of jobs and hoover up vast amounts of energy and water became a factor in the recent midterm elections in the US.

Many areas in the US have passed moratoriums on data centre development and many candidates deemed too close to big tech found that association a political liability rather than an asset. Many Democrat candidates publicly opposed data centre development and won.

Added to that, the Pied Piper of AI, chip maker Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, resigned last week after admitting to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

Dario Amodei is one of Silicon Valley's big beasts

Huang's spectacular fall from grace and Nvidia's 30% fall in value from its peak have left AI investors looking for a new champion for the AI boom that has underpinned the US stock market and arguably much of its entire economy for the last two years.

If Anthropic â and its CEO are to be AI's new Pied Piper, he will be playing a different tune to the relentlessly upbeat, leather jacket-clad talisman Huang.

While Huang wanted AI development to continue full throttle, Anthropic was built on misgivings over the levels of safety and responsibility the OpenAI leavers felt were being exercised over the development of society-changing technology.

Amodei has warned that AI capabilities could enable biological weapon development, mass surveillance, authoritarianism and the replacement of millions of white-collar, tax-paying workers.

He has called for a slowdown in its development as its powers are growing at such an exponential rate that it could very soon elude any human oversight.

That said, his paternalistic overtures have been sceptically received by those who point out that the pace of Anthropic's development of new more powerful models has shown no sign of the slowdown he's advocating for the rest of the industry.

But for all the concerns, just as with Elon Musk's SpaceX before it â the hype and the FOMO (fear of missing out) on an undoubtedly profound moment in technological and perhaps human development â means that buying interest is high.

It's reported that orders to buy shares exceed the number on offer by over six times.

With demand outstripping supply by such a margin, a first-day jump is likely given the oversubscription, but it doesn't tell you much about long-run return.

Using SpaceX as an example again, shares initially rose nearly 70% from its IPO sale price of $135 to $225. Shares in SpaceX are currently back to $145.

The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful and blow a bit of fresh new air into what some maintain still looks a lot like a bubble.

Eileen Burbidge, founder of tech investors Passion Capital, is one of those who reject the notion that AI is a bubble about to burst.

"Maybe the growth of the bubble has slowed down a little bit, but actually I wouldn't be willing to say that it's about to burst yet," says Burbidge. "I think this IPO could actually mean everyone could be off to the races again."

Fiona Cincotta believes Anthropic's IPO is about far more than just Anthropic. "I think what's going to be really interesting here is I think it can actually be used as a valuation benchmark for the entire AI ecosystem," she says.

"That's why I don't think this is just going to affect the share price of Anthropic. I think this is going to have much wider implications across the whole AI trade."

Two things can be true at the same time. The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful - FOMO is a powerful emotion in financial markets. But its probable success won't soothe the unease in some quarters that not every company, every investor and every citizen will think of AI as the answer to humanity's problems.

#109. Investigators make progress in hunt for cause of FAA ground stop

Engineers and analysts searching for the root cause of the technical issues behind Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration have been able to replicate the situation that was overwhelming parts of the air traffic system, people familiar with the investigation told CNBC.

While replicating the issue in a lab in Florida is progress, those investigating the matter have yet to determine exactly why two routers in question failed, according to the people, who requested anonymity to talk about an ongoing situation. Finding the root cause is critical to the FAA determining if there may be an issue with other routers that form the backbone of communications for the national air transportation network.

The ground stop lasted for more than four hours Friday, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford have said there is no indication the routers failed due to a cyberattack.

The incident in question happened shortly after L3Harris Technologies, the primary contractor supporting the FAA telecommunications network, installed a new router at a facility in El Dorado, Arkansas.

L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

Investigators said within eight minutes of its installation, the router began to transmit too much data within the FAA network.

That surge of data, 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds, destabilized aircraft communications and radar feeds, Duffy said.

Since the ground stop, Duffy has increased the amount of money he wants to upgrade the national air traffic system. After originally asking for $30 billion to build a digital network that will incorporate artificial intelligence, Duffy has now asked Congress for $50 billion.

#107. AI giant Anthropic valued at up to $1.5tn ahead of stock market debut

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has quickly become a big name in the tech industry

Anthropic has seen its value jump to as much as $1.5tn (Â£1.14tn) as its shares are set to start trading on the stock market on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the popular chatbot Claude said its shares will be priced at $83 each when it lists on the Nasdaq, a tech-focused stock exchange based in New York.

That will make it one of the most valuable companies in the world.

Anthropic's stock market debut will make it the first US firm focused exclusively on AI to go public. It will also mark a crucial test for the fast-growing industry, which faces questions over whether the huge amounts of money being poured into the technology will be profitable.

The firm said earlier this month it would offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each, which valued the company at up to $1.3tn.

But demand for Anthropic's stock debut - commonly referred to as its initial public offering, or IPO - is very high, according to one early investor in the company who spoke with the BBC on condition of anonymity.

If that turns out to be the case, Anthropic's valuation could quickly blow past its target on Thursday when trading begins.

Depending on how strong that demand is, Anthropic could even exceed the early market valuation of Elon Musk's SpaceX, which was the world's most highly valued stock debut when it went public with a value of $2.2tn in June.

However, in the weeks that followed, SpaceX shares fell significantly in price.

As it stands, Anthropic's listing will see the firm raise more than $68bn from its initial investors. It is also expecting another $26bn from private placements. So the total amount the company will raise is expected to be $94bn.

The amount of cash Anthropic has raised from its listing is already more than SpaceX raised in June.

OpenAI, the maker of ChatGPT and Anthropic's biggest AI rival, is expected to go public next year.

Until then, Anthropic will be the only public company in the US that is entirely focused on the technology, or one that was founded and built around AI tools and products. Its scope is different from tech giants like Google and Microsoft that also develop AI but are involved in other tech services.

The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

There is excitement around Anthropic's listing, but also some concern from investors around its ability to generate revenue and profits going forward.

Yang Wang, a principle financial analyst with Counterpoint Research, said the central issue for Anthropic is whether its business "can keep pace" as hundreds of billions of dollars in financial obligations come due in the next couple of years.

"Nevertheless, it is not an everyday occurrence for public investors to gain direct exposure to a frontier-model developer with the potential to shape the next stage of productivity," Wang said.

While nearly every other major tech company is pouring huge amounts of money into the technology and infrastructure required for their own AI tools, they are built around social media or online retail or online search. AI tools and applications are secondary.

"Anthropic going public gives us a whole new window into what AI demand looks like," Marta Norton, who heads strategy at investment firm Empower, told the BBC.

Anthropic was started by a group of co-founders, led by siblings Dario Amodei and Daniela Amodei, all of whom left their work at OpenAI to start a new AI firm.

The company has carved itself out in the ongoing AI race as more focused on building the technology with safety top of mind.

It refused to comply with a demand by the US Department of Defense that Anthropic's tools be used in an essentially unrestricted way.

CEO Dario Amodei has been outspoken in his belief that AI tools may soon be so powerful that they present a threat to human existence.

The company has urged developers to slow the development of AI, arguing that advanced systems could pose "catastrophic" risks to humanity if they are not handled with appropriate safeguards.

Rival firm OpenAI has also voiced similar concerns about the technology's risks and delayed its plans to list on the stock market to address AI safety concerns.

Nonetheless, Anthropic is looking to grow quickly and gain more access to financing through the public markets in order to do so.

Claude is Anthropic's widely-used AI chatbot

Norton said Anthropic going public now, in a time of market volatility comes down to the simple fact that the company needs access to more money.

"AI is financially demanding," Norton said. "The public markets open up the financial spigot a little bit more, and so the time has come."

But broader anxieties have started to grow about the AI industry's business model, which is reliant on expensive computer chips that are in short supply. The technology is also dependent on controversial data centres and funding such operations require deals with other tech companies.

The Federal Reserve Bank has expressed concern about the rapid growth of AI-related debt being financed through "complex and opaque" structures, according to minutes from its October meeting to discuss a potential rate rise.

A few of the meeting's participants also noted that "AI-related exposures were becoming increasingly concentrated among a small number of firms".

#104. Nvidia interim CEO Kress tells employees ‘let’s move forward’ at first all-hands meeting since Jensen Huang exit

Interim Nvidia CEO Colette Kress told employees "let's move forward" on Wednesday during the company's first all-hands meeting since founder Jensen Huang resigned, a person who attended the meeting told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because the meeting was private.

Kress was joined by newly appointed board chair Harvey Jones and hyperscale and high-performance computing head Ian Buck.

Several other senior members of Nvidia's executive team spoke or joined Kress on stage, including longtime company veteran Jeff Fisher, senior vice president of software engineering Dwight Diercks, senior vice president of GPU engineering Jonah Alben, executive vice president in charge of operations Debora Shoquist and co-founder Chris Malachowsky, according to people familiar with the matter.

Kress opened with the message about moving forward and also mentioned pay raises and explained that the yearly review process, when employees typically get pay raises and are granted more stock, would be moved up from March to December. Nvidia shares are down 37% from their 52-week high.

Jones discussed the search for the next CEO, the person who attended the meeting said. The meeting lasted about an hour and was in person as well as streamed on Microsoft Teams.

The general sentiment swirling in the room, according to the person, was that employees want Huang to come back. Employees have spoken up on X in recent days to call for the return of Huang as CEO, and one senior employee reportedly sent a letter to the board warning Nvidia would lose "irreplaceable talent" if it doesn't consider reinstating Huang.

But another person familiar with the matter disagreed, saying that employees welcomed the remarks from leadership as well as the visibility into company leadership. The person asked not to be named because the matter is private.

"The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," Jones said during the company's earnings call on Tuesday.

Some executives shared that they do not agree with Jones. "Why make such a strong definitive statement when the investigation is still playing out," an employee told CNBC. The employee asked not to be named given the sensitivity of the subject.

The meeting was held one day after Huang said he was ready to return as CEO should the board want him. Multiple employees told CNBC that Huang's letter "fired employees up."

Despite recent turmoil, Wednesday's meeting was not out of the ordinary. Nvidia typically holds all-hands the morning after quarterly earnings reports, according to a former employee, who asked not to be named because the matter is private. Kress recapped Tuesday's earnings report, calling it a "great quarter," according to the person who said employees welcomed the remarks and visibility from leadership.

In past meetings, Huang took time to answer questions submitted by employees, according to the former employee. A person familiar with the matter told CNBC that the executives also answered pre-submitted questions in Wednesday's all-hands.

Nvidia shares were down Wednesday, a day after the company reported third-quarter earnings that topped Wall Street expectations and gave no signal that the company was going to slow its growth.

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that executives at Nvidia's all-hands meeting answered employee questions. A previous version mischaracterized how executives handled employee questions.
