#101. McDonald's sex abuse: 'They were waiting for the younger girls to turn 18'

A former McDonald's worker who says a manager failed to protect her when she was being sexualised by customers, has called on the firm to âchange the way they treat younger employeesâ.

Hope, who worked at a branch in the West Midlands in 2023, also told BBC News she saw older colleagues eyeing up 16- and 17-year-old workers, and talking about wanting to sleep with them when they turned 18.

Hope is one of hundreds of workers bringing a discrimination claim against McDonald's. This week, a judge ruled against McDonald's attempt to have their claims struck out, a move that Hope welcomed.

McDonald's said: "Ensuring a safe, respectful and inclusive working environment across our restaurants and offices remains a fundamental priority."

The latest developments come after a BBC investigation in 2023 which heard that workers, some as young as 17, were being groped and harassed.

Last year, McDonald's staff told us they still faced sexual abuse and harassment.

McDonald's workers speak out over sexual abuse claims

McDonald's boss on abuse claims: 'I don't want to talk about the past'

Every McDonald's warned over staff sexual abuse

Hope, who is now 22, says that her branch of McDonalds had a very sexualised, "toxic" environment.

She spoke of a time a manager came up behind her and put his hands on her shoulders. From then on, whenever she saw him coming over, she says she "moved quick" to get away from him.

Ex-McDonald's worker: "You need to fix it so it doesn't happen again"

Another time, she says a manager failed to protect her when she says she was being sexualised and objectified by two customers on a night shift.

Hope says she told the manager what was happening, but he told her to "just suck it up and deal with it".

Her main concern, however, was for the younger teenagers, who she says weren't being looked after.

"You could tell there were other staff members waiting for their 18th birthdays. That sort of like toxic culture where it's like, 'Oh, you know, it's not illegal now.' It was that feeling from quite a few of them," she says.

"And why would you? That is literally a child still."

Hundreds of current and former McDonald's employees are bringing a discrimination claim against the firm.

The fast-food chain had attempted to have the complaints thrown out but this week at the London Central Employment Tribunal, Judge Glennie ruled that 450 cases can go ahead and they are now expected to be heard at the Employment Tribunal in April.

The BBC understands that these relate to indirect discrimination, not sexual harassment.

Hope told us she was not taking part in the legal case for money, saying they didn't know if they were going to get anything.

Instead, she said it was to try and "change the way they treat younger employees".

"It's going to change the way they treat allegations. It's going to shake up the whole internal process and make life easier for the kids who are now having their first jobs. And it's going to make itâ¯â¯a lot safer, I think, as well.â¯â¯And that's something you can't put a price on, the safety of the young these days.''

She also accused McDonald's of trying to "delay the inevitable" by attempting to have the claims thrown out.

"And the judge quite rightly looked at them and said: 'No. We're not going to do that.'"

The claims were presented by law firm Leigh Day, which is acting for the current and former employees.

The claims focus on the vulnerability of young or female workers in the workplace and follow allegations of widespread harassment and discrimination across McDonald's restaurants in the UK.

Leigh Day partner Kiran Daurka told the BBC: "We're really delighted that the judge has found that these claims can go forward to a full hearing. It's really important for access to justice that these cases are heard."

A McDonald's spokesperson said they could not comment on the specifics of ongoing legal proceedings.

In a statement, they said the company had "embedded an extensive set of robust and far-reaching initiatives" as part of its "steadfast commitment" to its staff, adding that this work continues.

"Any form of harassment, discrimination or inappropriate behaviour is unacceptable and has no place in our restaurants or offices. We are confident that with the guidance and partnership of external experts, we and our franchisees have industry leading safeguarding measures in place," the spokesperson added.

#100. Walmart is set to report earnings before the bell. Here's what to expect

Walmart is scheduled to report its third fiscal quarter earnings Thursday as the retailer prepares for the holiday season with an increased emphasis on value amid lower consumer sentiment.

The big-box retailer has posted solid growth over the past few quarters as it sees customers gravitate to reduced prices, improvements in delivery and gains in its advertising business. It's also leaned into attracting higher-income consumers with its Walmart+ membership.

Here's what the company is expected to report for its fiscal third quarter, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Walmart previously said it expects third-quarter net sales to climb 3% to 3.75%. CFO John David Rainey told CNBC last quarter that Walmart's "business is strong" and that he's encouraged by its progress.

The company is typically well insulated from macroeconomic pressures because of its value reputation. Last quarter, the company said it was lowering prices of products across multiple categories, including beef. It also saw a significant boost from tariff refunds, which Rainey said Walmart would use to cut prices.

In October, Walmart announced it was launching a joint membership option with its club subsidiary, Sam's Club, as it leans further into convenience. Both stores will still offer standalone memberships.

The retailer is also rolling out technology in its stores to improve the customer experience, including light-up digital shelf labels that are connected to its app. Those shelf labels and Walmart's AI push have also sparked concerns that the retailer could try to change prices based on demand and shoppers' personal data, which Walmart has denied.

Analysts from UBS wrote in a note last week that they believe the retailer's fiscal third-quarter results will show healthy market share gains.

"E-commerce continues to scale, the company's higher-margin alternative revenue streams continue to grow and Walmart is increasingly able to fund investments in price and convenience while still producing attractive operating income growth," the analysts wrote.

They added that they expect Walmart's fourth-quarter outlook to include "robust operating income growth."

Shares of Walmart have fluctuated this year but are up roughly 13% year-to-date.

#95. Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi received a subdued welcome on her first trip to China as premier, signaling Beijing's limited appetite for repairing ties strained by a simmering row over Taiwan.

At gatherings such as this week's Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, protocol details — including who greets a visiting leader at the airport and where they are positioned in official photographs — often convey political messages.

Takaichi was met on the tarmac Wednesday by a retired Chinese diplomat, rather than a serving official, and Japan's ambassador to China, Kenji Kanasugi.

The low-key reception contrasted sharply with the warmer welcomes given to other visiting leaders, including South Korean President Lee Jae Myung, while U.S. President Donald Trump was greeted personally by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

In the leaders' group photo, Takaichi was placed in the back row, off to the side and several places from Xi, who stood alongside Trump and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

"Beijing sought to downgrade her official status to that of a head of government rather than a head of state," said Jeremy Chan, senior analyst at Eurasia Group.

During Thursday morning's greeting line, Takaichi spent several minutes trying to talk to the Chinese leader without an interpreter present, while most leaders exchanged handshakes and kept walking.

"It has become increasingly difficult for her to engage in formal or even informal conversations with Xi," said Kei Koga, an associate professor at Nanyang Technological University in Singapore, who sees Takaichi's intention as to still "project the image that she could still communicate with him despite the ongoing tensions."

Takaichi's subdued reception signaled Beijing's lack of enthusiasm to repair ties with Tokyo, analysts said, potentially prolonging China's curbs on critical mineral exports that have gripped Japanese industries for the past year.

"China's frosty reception of Takaichi shows quite clearly that Beijing is not yet ready to forgive her over her remarks about Taiwan," Chan said, expecting the bilateral relationship to remain strained until Takaichi leaves office.

That could leave Japan grappling with continued restrictions on rare earth supplies from China next year, said Tianchen Xu, senior economist at the Economist Intelligence Unit.

Relations deteriorated sharply after Takaichi said in November 2025 that a Taiwan Strait crisis could constitute a situation threatening Japan's survival and warrant a self-defense response.

While the substance broadly aligned with Japan's existing security policy, previous prime ministers had generally avoided stating it so explicitly in a Taiwan scenario.

China reacted angrily and demanded that Takaichi retract the remarks. She has not done so, while stating that the door to dialogue with Beijing remains open, with minimal high-level contact over the past year.

China has since tightened restrictions affecting Japan, including controls on dual-use and rare earth exports with potential military uses. It also discouraged its citizens from traveling to Japan and halted Japanese seafood imports.

Takaichi's visit marked the first trip to China by a Japanese prime minister since Shinzo Abe traveled to Chengdu in December 2019 for a trilateral summit with Chinese and South Korean leaders.

No formal Takaichi-Xi meeting has been publicly scheduled during her visit. Meanwhile, Xi is expected to join Trump and Putin for a historic trilateral gathering on Thursday, alongside a bilateral meeting with Trump.

Takaichi needs to show a "reflection of what she said about Taiwan a year ago," said Huiyao Wang, founder of the Beijing-based Center for China and Globalization, which is often seen as close to Beijing's thinking.

Wang said Tokyo should show greater flexibility, pointing to Trump's comments that the U.S. would avoid getting involved in the Taiwan issue. He argued that Japan holding firm on the issue would not help relations with Beijing.

Such reflection, he said, "would really help to clarify some of those misunderstandings between the two countries."

#81. Musk accuses Zelenskyy of lying about Starshield restrictions in Ukraine

SpaceX founder Elon Musk accused Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy of being untruthful about restrictions around Ukraine's Starshield network and said Kyiv wanted to use Starlink for offensive operations.

Asked at The New York Times' Dealbook Summit about Zelenskyy's claims that Starshield had been slowed down, Musk said, "That's false ... I don't want to blow your mind, but Zelenskyy is not always truthful."

When pressed on whether he meant Ukraine was not always truthful, Musk doubled down with a reply: "No, it's not always truthful."

His comments came after Zelenskyy on Wednesday said speed restrictions remained in place on Starshield, hampering Ukraine's ability to intercept Russian missile and drone strikes. "We are discussing this with the American side," he said.

Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022, stoking a conflict that has resulted in a refugee crisis and hundreds of thousands of deaths. Ukraine has been able to beat back Russian forces more effectively than many observers thought possible, despite having a much smaller land area and population.

However, it relies heavily on SpaceX's Starlink and Starshield for capabilities like battlefield communications and drone operations. Musk has previously said that "if we were to turn off Starlink, the entire Ukrainian front would collapse immediately."

The SpaceX CEO sought to distinguish between Starlink, a commercial satellite network, and Starshield, a military system developed by SpaceX and operated by the U.S. government.

Musk said that Starlink is subject to speed limits because it is not intended for offensive military operations, adding that such missions should instead use the Starshield network.

"Any action taken by Starshield is up to the War Department. Starlink is commercial and is not intended as a weapon of war," Musk said, referring to the U.S. Department of Defense.

In October, Musk said the U.S. government and President Donald Trump were responsible for any restrictions on Ukraine's Starshield network after slowdowns to the service were reported.

"Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the U.S. government. Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS," Musk wrote on X.

The Wall Street Journal later reported on Oct. 20 that Musk had ordered the slowdown, citing U.S. officials and people familiar with the matter.

"What I'm trying to tell you is Zelenskyy is actually asking to use the Starlink commercial satellites for events of military actions, and we have declined to do so, as we have declined to do so from the beginning of this conflict," Musk added in the interview.

On Oct. 24, Musk said SpaceX had restricted Ukraine's access to Starlink because Kyiv had violated the service's terms by using it for offensive drone strikes, a claim Ukrainian officials disputed.

"Ukraine military violated Starlink terms of service and used it for offensive drone strikes to kill people. That loophole was closed last week," Musk wrote on X one day after.

CNBC has reached out to Ukraine's ministry of foreign affairs for comment but did not receive a response. The president's office could not immediately be reached.
